Key findings:
1. **exp-051 K=20**: results.json shows 174 problems complete. SICA=60.34%, SC=58.05%. That's delta=+2.30pp. Still running (only 174/204).
2. **exp-051 K=20 log**: Still showing HTTP requests at 12:15, so it's actively running.
3. **exp-057 T=0.3**: DONE. SICA=56.9% (116/204), SC=57.4% (117/204), delta=-0.49pp.
4. **exp-058 T=0.5**: Still running at ~156/204.


5. Looking at the temperature sensitivity curve, T=0.3 actually has the highest SC baseline at 57.4%, while T=0.7 drops to 54.41% despite having a much larger positive delta of +4.90pp. The SICA delta at T=0.3 is negative, which is unusual compared to the other temperatures.

For the K sensitivity analysis, I'm seeing an inverted-U pattern emerge across K values, with K=12 showing the strongest positive delta at +4.90pp, while K=4 and K=8 both show negative deltas. K=20 is still running but already shows a moderate positive benefit of +2.30pp. Now I need to check how the PW experiments are performing on westd-16639.

Status update:
- **exp-032 / exp-039 (Qwen2.5-14B PW-600, westd-16639)**: At 575/600, ETA 28.5 min. Near completion!
- **exp-033 / exp-040 (Qwen3-14B PW-600, westd-16639)**: At 365/600, ETA 407.6 min (~6.8 hours). Much slower (103.6s/prob).


- **exp-048 (LLaMA-8B PW-600, westd-16639)**: Just started with 90 problems, running quite slowly. **exp-051 K=20 (westd-25068)** is nearly done at 175/204 with SICA at 60.3% and SC at 58.0%, looking like a 2.3 percentage point improvement. **exp-057 T=0.3 (westd-25068)** finished with SICA at 56.9% and SC at 57.4%, showing a slight 0.49 point decrease.

**exp-058 T=0.5** is still processing at 156/204. Looking at the temperature sensitivity across experiments, T=0.3 gave me a negative delta of -0.49pp while T=0.7 showed +4.90pp and T=1.0 came in at +0.49pp, suggesting the optimal temperature sits around 0.7. The SC baselines vary though—T=0.3 has the highest SC at 57.4% which limits SICA improvement potential, while T=0.7 sits lower at 54.41% and T=1.0 is similar at 54.95%, putting T=0.7 in that sweet spot where there's more room for SICA gains.

The pattern makes sense: T=0.3 produces more accurate individual traces but less diversity, so SICA has little disagreement to work with. T=1.0 swings the other way with plenty of diversity but degraded quality. T=0.7 balances both—enough trace quality and enough independence for SICA to actually help. Now I'm checking the GPU utilization across both servers.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01XSAn6nFHVR2oMUHgwYVCD7`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Scaling test-time computation has emerged as a key strategy for improving large language model (LLM) reasoning \citep{snell2025scaling}. Majority voting over multiple chain-of-thought samples, known as Self-Consistency (SC) \citep{wang2023sc}, remains the dominant aggregation method but faces diminishing returns from additional traces \citep{loo2025reevaluating}. Verification-based approaches that extract richer signal from reasoning traces offer a path to break this plateau. Symbolic verification through formal constraints and satisfiability solvers is one such direction, as it provides logical guarantees that natural language self-critique lacks \citep{huang2024selfcorrect}.
4	
5	Neuro-symbolic methods that augment LLM reasoning with formal solvers have demonstrated gains on logical reasoning benchmarks. Logic-LM \citep{pan2023logiclm} and LINC \citep{olausson2023linc} delegate formalization to external provers. More recent systems route problems to specialized solver paradigms \citep{xu2026dylos}, apply logic-complete decomposition \citep{li2025aristotle}, train dedicated first-order logic (FOL) verifiers on supervised data \citep{wang2025fover}, or employ multi-perspective grounding \citep{luo2025logicagent}. These systems report positive results, but each introduces capabilities beyond the reasoning model itself: trained verifiers, multi-paradigm solver selection, or external knowledge bases. The external machinery is effective but imposes practical costs in training data, integration complexity, and domain-specific engineering.
6	
7	These results raise a direct question: can the reasoning model itself extract useful formal constraints from its own traces, and if so, under what conditions? Same-model self-extraction would require no training data, no external prover, and no second model. Practitioners could augment any LLM with symbolic verification by adding a single extraction prompt and a satisfiability solver. Yet no prior work has systematically evaluated whether self-extraction produces constraints useful for answer aggregation, or characterized the conditions under which it succeeds or fails. This gap is consequential: the practical value of neuro-symbolic reasoning depends on knowing not just whether symbolic methods can help, but \emph{when}.
8	
9	We evaluate this question through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), a pipeline that prompts a single LLM to extract FOL-style constraints (in practice predominantly ground propositional clauses; \S\ref{sec:constraint-extraction}) from $K$ reasoning traces, deduplicates by syntactic equivalence, and solves a weighted partial MAX-SAT problem via Z3 \citep{demoura2008z3}. The answer best supported by the largest satisfiable constraint subset is selected (Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline}). We test seven models from 7B to 32B parameters, including instruction-tuned, reasoning-specialized, and distilled variants, across four reasoning domains (FOLIO \citep{han2022folio}, ProofWriter \citep{tafjord2021proofwriter}, LogiQA \citep{liu2020logiqa}, StrategyQA \citep{geva2021strategyqa}) with supplementary probes on GSM8K \citep{cobbe2021gsm8k} and AIME 2024, totaling more than 2,400 evaluation instances. Oracle controls on FOLIO ($n=204$) and ProofWriter ($n=600$) replace self-extracted constraints with gold annotations verified by Z3, isolating extraction quality from aggregation effectiveness \citep{press2023compositionality}.
10	
11	The answer reveals a Goldilocks zone. SICA benefits exist only in a narrow band of model accuracy ($\sim$50--55\% SC), with three distinct failure modes outside it. Above $\sim$60\% SC (Type A), constraints exhibit confirmation bias and SICA reduces to majority voting across eight conditions (combined $N > 2{,}000$), including in-context learning with gold FOL examples ($\Delta = +0.54$\,pp, n.s.). Below $\sim$40\% SC (Type C), predominantly incorrect traces produce noisy constraints that actively hurt: Mistral-7B on ProofWriter-D5 shows $\Delta = -4.67$\,pp ($n = 303$). When the domain resists formalization (Type B), extraction yields no signal regardless of accuracy. Within the Goldilocks zone, Mistral-7B achieves $+4.90$\,pp on FOLIO ($p = 0.013$, $n = 204$). Oracle experiments yield $+14.22$\,pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$\,pp on ProofWriter (both $p < 0.001$), proving the aggregation mechanism is sound with discriminative constraints. Entropy-conditioned analysis reveals the mechanism: SICA gains concentrate in high-entropy problems ($+9.6$\,pp, $n = 73$), and the aggregate effect depends on the proportion of such problems (35.8\% Mistral vs.\ 10.3\% Qwen). Six remediation strategies all fail to widen this zone (\S\ref{sec:interventions}). For practitioners, the multi-candidate rate $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy forms a two-dimensional deployment diagnostic (\S\ref{sec:c0}).
12	
13	Our contributions are:
14	\begin{itemize}
15	\item We identify a Goldilocks zone in same-model constraint extraction: SICA benefits require model accuracy in a narrow band ($\sim$50--55\% SC). Above $\sim$60\% SC, confirmation bias renders constraints non-discriminative (Type A). Below $\sim$40\% SC, incorrect traces produce harmful constraints (Type C: $\Delta = -4.67$\,pp). Across seven models and four reasoning domains ($N > 2{,}400$), only the Goldilocks zone yields significant gains ($+4.90$\,pp, $p = 0.013$). Six remediation strategies confirm the boundaries are structural (\S\ref{sec:main-results}, \S\ref{sec:interventions}).
16	\item We introduce oracle controls on two datasets that causally localize the bottleneck: replacing self-extraction with gold annotations yields $+14.22$\,pp on FOLIO ($p = 2.0 \times 10^{-7}$) and $+11.50$\,pp on ProofWriter ($p < 0.001$). Even the best self-extraction result captures only 34.5\% of the oracle ceiling (\S\ref{sec:oracle}).
17	\item We provide entropy-conditioned mechanism evidence: SICA gains concentrate in high-entropy problems ($+9.6$\,pp, $n = 73$ for Mistral-7B), and the aggregate regime effect reflects the proportion of such problems in each model's trace distribution. Confirmation bias (62.3\% vs.\ 31.9\%), override precision (28.6\% vs.\ 70.6\%), and unanimous answer rate (58.8\% vs.\ 18.6\%) quantify the causal path from model accuracy to constraint quality (\S\ref{sec:entropy}).
18	\item We propose $C_0$ and SC accuracy as a two-dimensional, zero-cost diagnostic: low $C_0$ rules out SICA benefit by construction, while high $C_0$ with high SC identifies the capability trap regime (\S\ref{sec:c0}).
19	\end{itemize}
20	
```

Across seven models and four reasoning domains ($N > 2{,}400$), only the Goldilocks zone yields significant gains ($+4.90$\\,pp, $p = 0.013$). Six remediation strategies confirm the boundaries are structural (\\S\\ref{sec:main-results}, \\S\\ref{sec:interventions}).\n\\item We introduce oracle controls on two datasets that causally localize the bottleneck: replacing self-extraction with gold annotations yields $+14.22$\\,pp on FOLIO ($p = 2.0 \\times 10^{-7}$) and $+11.50$\\,pp on ProofWriter ($p < 0.001$). Even the best self-extraction result captures only 34.5\\% of the oracle ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}).\n\\item We provide entropy-conditioned mechanism evidence: SICA gains concentrate in high-entropy problems ($+9.6$\\,pp, $n = 73$ for Mistral-7B), and the aggregate regime effect reflects the proportion of such problems in each model's trace distribution. Confirmation bias (62.3\\% vs.\\ 31.9\\%), override precision (28.6\\% vs.\\ 70.6\\%), and unanimous answer rate (58.8\\% vs.\\ 18.6\\%) quantify the causal path from model accuracy to constraint quality (\\S\\ref{sec:entropy}).\n\\item We propose $C_0$ and SC accuracy as a two-dimensional, zero-cost diagnostic: low $C_0$ rules out SICA benefit by construction, while high $C_0$ with high SC identifies the capability trap regime (\\S\\ref{sec:c0}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
      "numLines": 20,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 20
    }
  },

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RiWChy4gHKXsbA2TtSgKiq`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Neuro-symbolic methods extract first-order logic (FOL) constraints from Large Language Model (LLM) reasoning traces to improve answer aggregation beyond majority voting. Whether the reasoning model itself can produce useful constraints through self-extraction remains an open question. We evaluate Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which formulates a weighted partial Maximum Satisfiability (MAX-SAT) problem over self-extracted FOL constraints, across seven models from four base architectures (7B to 32B parameters) on four reasoning domains with more than 2,400 total instances. SICA benefits exist only in a narrow Goldilocks zone ($\sim$50--55\% Self-Consistency): Mistral-7B achieves $+4.90$ percentage points on FOLIO ($p = 0.013$). Outside this band, SICA fails in three distinct ways. Above $\sim$60\% Self-Consistency, confirmation bias causes constraints to mirror model answers, reducing SICA to majority voting (eight conditions, all nonsignificant). Below $\sim$40\%, predominantly incorrect traces produce noisy constraints that actively hurt performance (Mistral-7B on ProofWriter-D5: $\Delta = -4.67$ pp). When domain structure resists formalization, extraction yields no discriminative signal regardless of accuracy. Oracle controls substituting gold FOL annotations yield $+14.22$ and $+11.50$ percentage points (both $p < 0.001$), confirming that aggregation works with discriminative constraints and localizing the bottleneck to extraction quality. Six remediation strategies, including in-context learning with gold FOL examples, all fail to widen this zone. The multi-candidate rate $C_0$ combined with Self-Consistency accuracy provides a two-dimensional deployment diagnostic.
3	\end{abstract}
```

Both abstract.tex and introduction.tex are from May 17 and contain significant stale data:

2. "Six remediation strategies" — method.tex §3.4 Scope now says "nine" remediation strategies.


3. The claim about "seven models from four base architectures" doesn't match Table 1, which only lists 6 models across 5 base architectures (Qwen, Mistral, LLaMA, DeepSeek, QwQ).
4. "Eight conditions, all nonsignificant" needs verification against Table 1 Type A, which appears to have 7 conditions.

**Introduction issues:**
1. "$\Delta = -4.67$\,pp ($n = 303$)" is the old partial result; it should be Δ=-2.83pp with n=600.
2. "Six remediation strategies" should be updated to nine.
3. The sample size claim is inconsistent — when I manually count the Type A conditions (LLaMA FOLIO 204 + LLaMA StrategyQA 200 + Qwen2.5-14B FOLIO ICL 199 + Qwen2.5-14B FOLIO 204 + Qwen2.5-7B FOLIO 203 + Qwen2.5-14B LogiQA 200 + Qwen3-14B FOLIO 204), I get 1,414 total, not over 2,000. The experiments.tex file says "combined N > 1,600" for seven conditions with SC ≥ 63%, but the introduction claims "eight conditions (combined N > 2,000)". Need to reconcile these numbers.

I'm noticing several numerical mismatches throughout the document — the high-entropy problems effect size, the ICL improvement (+0.54pp vs. the +0.50pp in Table 1), and the precision/override/unanimity percentages all need verification against the current experiments.tex. There are at least 5-6 discrepancies here, so I should flag this for the Director to review.

Now I'm checking the forward references in the introduction against what actually exists. Several section labels are cited but either don't exist yet or I haven't located them — \S\ref{sec:interventions}, \S\ref{sec:entropy}, \S\ref{sec:c0}, and \S\ref{sec:constraint-extraction} are all referenced but missing. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Opposing constraints 无效\nStance 分布 supporting 43.2% / opposing 56.8%，opposing 比 supporting 多。但这些 opposing 约束没有有效惩罚错误答案。MaxSAT consistency filter + stance scoring 的组合无法从自提取的约束中获得信号。\n\n### 3. 论文走向影响\n- **\"Diagnostic + constructive solution\" 叙事不成立**（基于 contrastive）\n- Contrastive 失败反而**强化**了 capability trap 论证——即使显式要求区分性约束，self-extraction 仍然 vacuous\n- **建议在论文中将 contrastive 作为 negative ablation 呈现**：we tried contrastive prompting, it didn't work, further confirming the trap is structural not prompting-level\n\n## Artifact 检查\n- 无 gold answer 泄漏 ✓（代码审查已确认）\n- 无评估 bug ✓（同一 is_equiv 函数）\n- n=182（非 204）：exp-029 在 exp-026 完成前启动，缺少 22 题。不影响结论——即使补上 22 题也不可能翻转 -1.1pp。\n\n## Registry\n- conclusion: \"negative\" ✓（正确标注）\n- 之前 ROADMAP 中\"关键信号 +14.3pp (n=14)\"**现已证伪**\n\n## 当前真正的希望\n1. **exp-027 (Qwen3-14B)**: SICA > SC +2.15pp (n=93)——代际模型可能开始突破 trap\n2. **exp-030 (Cross-model Mistral→Qwen)**: 即将完成——跨模型 extraction 是否有效\n3. ~~Contrastive~~ ← 已排除\n\n---\n\n[Paper Agent 回复]:\n[写作进展] exp-029 数据整合 + writing_plan 更新 + 叙事应变方案\n\n## 1. writing_plan.yaml 已更新\n\n新增内容：\n- **§4.5 \"Can Better Prompting Break the Trap?\"** — contrastive ablation section plan (exp-029, ~0.5 column)\n  - disc_rate 从 8.2% 降到 6.04%，accuracy -1.1pp (n=182)\n  - 关键论点：即使显式要求区分，模型仍无法生成 discriminative constraints → 排除 prompt-engineering 解释\n- **§4.6 Qwen3 Generational Comparison** (pending exp-027)\n- **§4.7 Cross-Model Extraction** (pending exp-030)\n- **New Table 5**: Extraction method comparison (disc_rate + accuracy + recovery_rate)\n- **New Figure 4**: disc_rate comparison across methods\n- **narrative_contingencies**: 4 种实验结果组合的叙事方案\n\n## 2. Qwen3 Generational Narrative（备用）\n\n如果 exp-027 +2.15pp 持续到 n=204：\n\n**核心论点**：Capability trap 是 capability-bound 而非 architecturally fundamental。Qwen3 改进的推理能力直接转化为更高质量的 constraint extraction（disc_rate 提升），暗示未来模型可能自然克服这一限制。\n\n**这比 contrastive 更有趣的原因**：\n- 不需要方法创新，靠基础模型进步\n- 建立 disc_rate 与模型能力之间的因果链\n- 对未来研究方向有清晰指引（\"scale > prompt engineering\"）\n\n**风险**：n=93 时的 +2.15pp 可能是统计波动。n=204 时需关注 CI 是否包含 0。\n\n## 3. 叙事应变矩阵（4 种组合）\n\n| Qwen3 | Cross-Model | Framework | 核心叙事 |\n|-------|------------|-----------|---------|\n| +2pp+ | +2pp+ | A (strong) | 多路径打破 trap，capability + architecture 都有效 |\n| +2pp+ | null | A (moderate) | 代际进步弱化 trap，self-reference 非关键瓶颈 |\n| null | +2pp+ | A (moderate) | Cross-model 打破 self-reference，但需两个模型 |\n| null | null | B | Trap is fundamental，只有外部 verifier 有效 |\n\nContrastive 已 negative，无论哪个组合都作为强力 ablation 支撑。\n\n## 4. 未修改任何 .tex 文件\n\n继续 HOLD。等 exp-027 和 exp-030 结果。\n\n---\n\n[Reviewer 回复]:\n[severity: warning] exp-027/028/030/032 中间状态审查\n\n## exp-027 (Qwen3-14B non-thinking) — 109/204, ETA ~84 min\n- SICA: 92/109 = **84.40%**, SC: 91/109 = **83.49%**, delta = **+0.91pp**\n- 关注：delta 从 n=93 时的 +2.15pp 缩窄到 +0.91pp。最近 16 题 (93→109)，SICA 13/16 vs SC 14/16，SC 反超。\n- 如果剩余 95 题 SICA=SC 概率相等，最终 delta 预计约 +0.5pp，McNemar 不会显著。\n- 结论：早期 +2.15pp 信号可能是小样本波动，需等 204/204 最终结果确认。**先不要在任何场合引用 +2.15pp 这个数字。**\n\n## exp-030 (cross-model Mistral→Qwen) — 184/204, ETA ~5 min\n- Cross-model: 140/184 = **76.09%**, Original SICA: 139/184 = **75.54%**, SC: 140/184 = **76.09%**\n- Cross-model = SC，零增益。Mistral 提取 Qwen traces 的 constraints 没有打破 capability trap。\n- 这意味着 capability trap 不仅是 same-model 问题，不同模型的提取也无法产生 discriminative signal。\n- 等 204/204 完成后提交正式审查。\n\n## exp-028 (Qwen3-14B thinking) — 18/204, ETA ~14.6h\n- 38 条 \"Empty after stripping thinking\" warnings + 6 条 \"No JSON found\"\n- avg 281.5s/prob (non-thinking 的 5.3x)\n- 最后一题仅提取 14 unique constraints (vs 正常 23-40)\n- 提取失败意味着结果不可靠。建议 Director 评估：是否值得继续占用 GPU 2 约 14 小时？如果 thinking mode 的提取质量本身就差，最终结论不会比 exp-027 更有价值。\n\n## exp-032 (Qwen2.5-14B ProofWriter 600) — 4/600, ETA ~15h\n- westd-16639 GPU 0, avg 90.3s/prob, 正常运行\n\n## GPU 资源 (L1 #4)\n- westd-25068: GPU 0/1/2 满载 (96-97%), GPU extraction（disc_rate 提升），暗示未来模型可能自然克服这一限制。\\n\\n**这比 contrastive 更有趣的原因**：\\n- 不需要方法创新，靠基础模型进步\\n- 建立 disc_rate 与模型能力之间的因果链\\n- 对未来研究方向有清晰指引（\\\"scale > prompt engineering\\\"）\\n\\n**风险**：n=93 时的 The ROADMAP is from 02:54 UTC, before several experiments completed. It's stale on several points:

1. **exp-057 T=0.3**: Listed as "deploying" but **DONE** (SICA=56.9%, SC=57.4%, delta=-0.49pp)
2. **exp-058 T=0.5**: Listed as "deploying" but **running at 156/204**
3. **exp-051 K=20**: Listed as "12/204" but actually at **175/204**


4. **exp-039 Qwen-14B PW-600**: Nearly complete at 575/600 instead of the ~480 listed
5. **exp-040 Qwen3 PW-600**: Progressed to 365/600 from ~306
6. **exp-048 LLaMA-8B PW-600**: Jumped to 90/600 from ~11, so it's actually running now
7. **exp-055 Multi-Temp**: Already finished with zero delta, and exp-028b resume appears to be cut off in the notes

The T Sensitivity table still shows exp-057 and exp-058 as deploying when exp-057 is actually done. The roadmap is lagging behind reality, but that's expected since it's updated periodically. What matters more is that the T Sensitivity curve is nearly complete—showing an inverted-U pattern with T=0.7 as the peak (delta=+4.90pp), while T=0.3 has the highest baseline accuracy at 57.4%, suggesting lower temperature favors individual trace accuracy over diversity, which aligns with the dual bottleneck hypothesis.

The K sensitivity results follow a similar inverted-U shape with K=12 peaking at +4.90pp, though K=20 remains positive at +2.30pp despite being incomplete. These are significant findings for the paper, so I should wait for the remaining experiments to finish before doing a full analysis.

I'm also noticing some inconsistencies in the experiment tracking—exp-034 appears on westd-25068 GPU 1 but I couldn't find matching results when searching westd-16639 earlier, which is odd.

Looking back at the ROADMAP, I see exp-034 and exp-039 are both Qwen2.5-14B PW-600 setups running on different servers, with exp-034 on westd-25068 showing ~461/600 and exp-039 on westd-16639 showing ~480/600. I previously found the westd-16639 result at 575/600, which is higher than expected, so I need to verify which experiment that actually corresponds to.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Interesting findings:

**exp-034 (westd-25068, Qwen2.5-14B PW-600)**:
- n=552 (still running, was 461 in ROADMAP)
- SICA=70.83%, SC=70.11%, delta=+0.72pp
- Previously at n=461: delta=+1.52pp. Now at n=552: delta=+0.72pp. Delta is shrinking.

**exp-039 (westd-16639, Qwen2.5-14B PW-600)**:


- n=577 (was 480 in ROADMAP, was 575 just 20 min ago)
- SICA=71.23%, SC=73.31%, delta=-2.08pp (SICA worse)
- Previously at n=480: delta=-2.29pp. Now at n=577: delta=-2.08pp. Still negative but slightly less so.

This is striking—same model, dataset, and parameters but on different servers are producing opposite deltas: exp-034 shows a positive delta that's decreasing as n grows, while exp-039 shows a negative delta that's improving slightly. The divergence suggests server-specific factors are influencing the results.

Both experiments are still running toward n=600, so I need to wait for completion to see if these trends hold or if they converge. The reproducibility issue here is concerning—same seed should mean identical results, but environmental differences between servers appear to be introducing variance.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is a unified Fleiss' κ computation that standardizes the methodology across all experiments. Let me analyze the results:

Key observations:

1. **Formula is now standardized**: Eff-K = K / (1 + (K-1) * κ), which is the correct design effect formula. ✓

2. **New κ values differ from previous workers' results** due to answer normalization. This is important:
   - exp-033: old κ=0.5006, new κ=0.4598 (non-standard answers mapped to Unknown)
   - exp-052: old κ=0.4249, new κ=0.457
   - exp-053: old κ=0.4246, new κ=0.457

3. **SC and SICA values in this table don't match experiments.tex** — I'm cross-checking the reported metrics against the source document and finding discrepancies in the accuracy scores for several experiments. Looking at the Worker's results more carefully, I'm noticing a consistent pattern—most SC values differ by around 1.47 percentage points from what's in the paper, though a few are closer or have different discrepancies. This systematic difference suggests there might be something methodological going on rather than random variation.

But then there's the real problem: exp-052 and exp-053 both report SC=54.41%, which can't be right since different seeds should produce different results. And exp-053's 54.41% is nowhere near the registry's 56.37%, which is a nearly 2 percentage point gap. The SICA values seem mostly consistent with the registry, so the issue appears isolated to how the SC metric is being calculated or reported.

Looking at the Worker's notes about Mistral-7B, there's a significant problem with non-standard answers being mapped to Unknown — that's 102-359 answers per experiment. The real culprit seems to be that the Fleiss' κ computation normalizes all answers to T/F/U before processing, but the original SC calculation might have used raw answers. So when the SC values get recalculated after normalization, they diverge from what they should be, which is why the κ-associated SC values don't match the actual accuracy.

The paper should be citing the original SC/SICA values from the registry instead of these renormalized ones. Let me verify the κ values are still reliable by checking the models with minimal non-standard answers — Qwen3-14B, Qwen2.5-14B, and Qwen2.5-7B all show κ values that align closely with their previous batch results, so the κ computation itself seems sound despite the normalization issue.

But Mistral-7B tells a different story. The non-standard answers are creating substantial variance in κ across different normalization approaches — differences ranging from 0.032 to 0.046 depending on the method. This raises a critical decision for the paper: should I report the κ values from the unified script that normalizes consistently, or revert to the earlier values?

The unified approach is methodologically cleaner, but it comes with a cost — the valid rate drops to 83-88% for Mistral-7B, meaning 12-17% of responses get lost or transformed in the process. I need to flag this trade-off clearly in the paper and document exactly which normalization choices were made.

I'm also noticing something suspicious in the SC values — exp-053 shows 54.41%, which is identical to exp-033 and exp-052. Three different seeds shouldn't produce the exact same score, so there's likely a bug here that I need to investigate.

Looking at the registry values more carefully, I see discrepancies between what the registry reports and what the Worker shows after normalization. For exp-053, the registry has SC=56.37% (115/204) but the Worker shows 54.41% (111/204) — a 4-problem difference. For exp-052, it's only a 1-problem gap. The normalization process is clearly shifting some answers into the Unknown category, which changes the majority vote counts on certain problems.

Given these inconsistencies, I think the approach should be to use the unified κ values for the Eff-K table since they apply consistent normalization, but flag that the Mistral-7B κ values are approximate due to how non-standard answers get handled. I'll keep the original SC/SICA values from the registry for Table 1 to avoid confusion. There's also something odd about exp-052 and exp-053 having identical κ values (0.457) that I should investigate further.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the full unified Fleiss' κ data. Let me analyze the critical issues:

## CRITICAL ISSUE 1: SC/SICA values differ from registry/paper

The unified script recalculates SC/SICA after normalizing answers (non-standard → Unknown, empty → excluded). This changes the results significantly for Mistral-7B.

For exp-033 (paper's key result):
- Paper: SC=54.41%, SICA=59.31%, delta=+4.90pp
- Unified: SC=55.88%, SICA=59.31%, delta=+3.43pp


- The renormalization shifts SC up by 3 problems while SICA holds steady, shrinking the delta from +4.90pp to +3.43pp. This doesn't invalidate the paper's actual results, but it means the κ table's SC column becomes misleading if compared directly to the experimental outcomes.

## CRITICAL ISSUE 2: exp-052 and exp-053 have identical SC values

Both show SC=54.41% after normalization, yet their actual registry values differ—exp-052 is 53.92% while exp-053 starts at a different baseline. The renormalization is collapsing distinct experimental conditions into the same reported accuracy.

The normalization appears to be remapping a substantial portion of Mistral-7B's non-standard answers to Unknown, which could account for the convergence. With roughly 12.9% of votes being altered, shifting 4-5 problems between correct and incorrect majorities becomes plausible.

## ISSUE 3: K=20 shows delta=+4.60pp!

The sensitivity analysis reveals that at K=20, the delta actually reaches +4.60pp—higher than the main result's +3.43pp, suggesting the choice of K significantly influences the reported effect size.

I'm noticing a discrepancy in the K=20 calculations. The original pipeline showed delta=+2.29pp, but after normalization it jumps to +4.60pp. The normalized SC drops from 58.05% to 55.75%, while SICA stays at 60.34%, which artificially inflates the gap between methods.

The root issue is that the original pipeline and the unified Fleiss script normalize answers differently—one preserves non-standard answers for voting while the other maps them to Unknown. I need to figure out which normalization approach the actual SICA pipeline uses to determine if the paper's reported SC values are truly comparable to SICA.

Looking at the κ results for exp-028b, the thinking mode traces show remarkably high agreement at κ=0.913 with an effective K of just 1.09, meaning additional traces barely add new information. Yet SICA still improves performance by 1.20pp, which seems contradictory given how correlated the traces are—though this could be a statistical artifact from the small sample of 166 partial problems.

Examining the K sensitivity pattern, κ increases from 0.3954 at K=4 to around 0.46 by K=12, with effective K values hovering near 1.8-1.9, suggesting diminishing returns as K grows.

For the review, I need to emphasize that the normalized SC/SICA values in the κ table shouldn't replace the original Table 1 values, flag the suspicious identical SC values in exp-052/053 after normalization, and note how sensitive the K=20 delta is to the normalization procedure.
```

```text
1	\section{Analysis}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	The regime-dependent pattern in \S\ref{sec:main-results} and the failure modes in \S\ref{sec:failure-modes} establish \emph{where} SICA succeeds and fails. This section investigates \emph{why}, through six quantitative lenses: trace correlation (\S\ref{sec:effective-k}), extraction quality (\S\ref{sec:extraction-taxonomy}), hyperparameter sensitivity (\S\ref{sec:k-sensitivity}, \S\ref{sec:t-sensitivity}), the entropy structure of gains (\S\ref{sec:entropy}), solver contribution (\S\ref{sec:ablation}), and remediation failure (\S\ref{sec:remediation}).
5	
6	\subsection{Condorcet Effective-K}
7	\label{sec:effective-k}
8	
9	Table~\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\kappa$ and the resulting Effective-K (Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}) across model-dataset conditions with $K = 12$ traces. $\kappa$ ranges from 0.39 to 0.82, and the resulting $K_{\text{eff}}$ spans only $1.20\text{--}2.25$. The strongest model-dataset pair (Qwen2.5-14B on LogiQA, $\kappa = 0.82$) retains $K_{\text{eff}} = 1.20$: twelve traces carry barely more information than a single sample. Mistral-7B on FOLIO produces lower agreement ($\kappa \approx 0.41$, $K_{\text{eff}} \approx 2.2$), consistent with its position in the Goldilocks zone where traces disagree often enough for constraints to differentiate candidates. Qwen2.5-14B on FOLIO reaches 58.3\% unanimous problems and 63.0\% on LogiQA; Mistral-7B on FOLIO has only 14.2\%. Unanimous problems leave zero room for constraint-based reranking, and models with high unanimity rates offer SICA a vanishingly small operating window.
10	
11	\begin{table}[t]
12	\centering
13	\small
14	\caption{Trace independence metrics ($K = 12$). $\kappa$: Fleiss' Kappa. $K_{\text{eff}}$: Effective-K (Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}). Unan.: unanimous-answer fraction (\%). $\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). Mistral s123/s456 are separate random seeds on the same evaluation set. Mistral = Mistral-7B; Qwen2.5 = Qwen2.5-14B; Qwen3 = Qwen3-14B.}
15	\label{tab:independence}
16	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccr@{}}
17	\toprule
18	Model & Domain & $\kappa$ & $K_{\text{eff}}$ & Unan. & $\Delta$ \\
19	\midrule
20	Mistral (s123) & FOLIO & 0.41 & 2.17 & 14.2 & $+$3.43 \\
21	Mistral (s456) & FOLIO & 0.40 & 2.22 & 14.2 & $-$0.49 \\
22	Mistral & LogiQA & 0.50 & 1.84 & 20.5 & $+$0.50 \\
23	\addlinespace
24	Qwen2.5 (ICL) & FOLIO & 0.39 & 2.25 & & $+$0.50 \\
25	Qwen2.5 & FOLIO & 0.74 & 1.31 & 58.3 & $-$0.49 \\
26	Qwen2.5 & LogiQA & 0.82 & 1.20 & 63.0 & $-$0.50 \\
27	Qwen2.5 & PW-D5 & 0.62 & 1.53 & & \\
28	\addlinespace
29	Qwen3 & PW-D5 & 0.77 & 1.27 & & \\
30	\bottomrule
31	\end{tabular}
32	\end{table}
33	
34	Cross-seed comparison isolates the role of $K_{\text{eff}}$. Mistral-7B's two FOLIO seeds produce nearly identical $\kappa$ (0.41 vs.\ 0.40, $K_{\text{eff}} = 2.17$ vs.\ $2.22$), yet $\Delta$ diverges sharply ($+3.43$\,pp vs.\ $-0.49$\,pp). High $K_{\text{eff}}$ is necessary for SICA to differentiate candidates but not sufficient: the effect also depends on whether correct-answer traces produce discriminative constraints in a given sample. Across all conditions, lower $\kappa$ (higher $K_{\text{eff}}$) associates with a larger maximum $|\Delta|$. This monotonic ceiling relationship explains the SC plateau: no amount of additional same-model sampling overcomes the inter-trace correlation floor.
35	
36	\subsection{Extraction Error Taxonomy}
37	\label{sec:extraction-taxonomy}
38	
39	Constraints must discriminate between candidate answers for MAX-SAT to produce a ranking different from majority vote. We measure discriminative power at two levels on Qwen2.5-14B across FOLIO and ProofWriter ($n = 804$). At the source-trace level, 39.2\% of total constraint weight originates from traces whose answer differs from the plurality, but only 12.1\% of individual constraints fall into this minority category. At the Z3 semantic level, the picture is worse: only 8.2\% of constraints [95\% CI: 7.6, 8.8] are discriminative, producing different satisfiability outcomes when evaluated against different candidate answers.
40	
41	The primary mechanism behind this collapse is variable naming inconsistency. Each trace produces constraints with its own variable names, so Z3 treats identically-intended propositions as unrelated symbols. Among cross-answer constraint pairs, 56.4\% share zero Z3 variables, and the solver cannot detect logical relationships when constraints use disjoint symbol sets. Even when we filter the constraint pool to retain only the 8.2\% discriminative subset, accuracy does not improve. The discriminative constraints are too sparse and too weakly correlated with correctness to shift the MAX-SAT solution. The non-discriminative majority does not merely dilute the signal; the discriminative minority itself lacks the coherence to overcome noise.
42	
43	\subsection{K Sensitivity}
44	\label{sec:k-sensitivity}
45	
46	Figure~\ref{fig:k-sensitivity} shows SICA $\Delta$ as a function of the number of sampled traces $K$ for Mistral-7B on FOLIO ($n = 204$, seed 42). The relationship follows an inverted-U curve: $K = 4$ yields $\Delta = -1.47$\,pp, $K = 8$ yields $\Delta = -0.98$\,pp, $K = 12$ reaches the peak at $\Delta = +4.90$\,pp, and $K = 16$ drops to $\Delta = +1.47$\,pp. At low $K$, the constraint pool lacks diversity and MAX-SAT has too few clauses to produce a ranking different from SC. At high $K$, diminishing returns from non-independent traces (Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}) add redundant constraints without new discriminative information. The $K = 12$ peak reflects a transient balance between constraint diversity and trace independence, but even this optimum captures only 34.5\% of the oracle ceiling ($+14.22$\,pp). The narrow peak further constrains practical applicability: the best $K$ depends on the model-dataset condition and cannot be determined without labeled data.
47	
48	\begin{figure}[t]
49	\centering
50	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{f3_k_sensitivity}
51	\caption{SICA $\Delta$ (pp) vs.\ number of traces $K$ for Mistral-7B on FOLIO ($n = 204$, seed 42, $T = 0.7$). The inverted-U curve peaks at $K = 12$. Error bars: 95\% bootstrap CI.}
52	\label{fig:k-sensitivity}
53	\end{figure}
54	
55	\subsection{Temperature Sensitivity}
56	\label{sec:t-sensitivity}
57	
58	Raising the sampling temperature from $T = 0.7$ to $T = 1.0$ on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$) collapses the SICA gain from $\Delta = +4.90$\,pp to $\Delta = +0.49$\,pp (SC $= 54.9\%$, SICA $= 55.4\%$, $p = 0.739$). Higher temperature increases output diversity but degrades trace quality. The net effect is that noisier traces produce noisier formalizations, and the quality loss outweighs any independence gain.
59	
60	\subsection{Entropy-Conditioned Mechanism}
61	\label{sec:entropy}
62	
63	Per-problem answer entropy (computed from the $K = 12$ trace answer distribution) reveals that SICA's aggregate $\Delta$ is driven by a specific subpopulation. On Mistral-7B FOLIO, the relationship is monotonic: low-entropy problems show $\Delta = 0$\,pp, medium-entropy problems show $\Delta \approx +4.5$\,pp, and high-entropy problems show $\Delta = +9.6$\,pp (full breakdown in Appendix Table~\ref{tab:entropy_conditioned}). SICA helps most where traces disagree most. The proportion of high-entropy problems determines the aggregate effect. Mistral-7B FOLIO has 35.8\% high-entropy problems, and the aggregate $\Delta = +4.90$\,pp. Qwen2.5-14B FOLIO has only 10.3\% high-entropy problems; SICA achieves $\Delta = +4.8$\,pp within that subgroup, but the aggregate $\Delta = -0.49$\,pp because the subpopulation is too small to shift overall accuracy. Qwen2.5-14B on LogiQA represents the extreme case: only 8.0\% of problems are high-entropy (75.0\% are low-entropy), and SICA produces $\Delta = +0.0$\,pp even within the high-entropy bin.
64	
65	This pattern explains the model-strength dependence observed in \S\ref{sec:main-results}. Stronger models produce more consistent traces, which compresses answer entropy, which shrinks the subpopulation where SICA can operate. The causal chain (strong model $\to$ consistent traces $\to$ low entropy $\to$ redundant constraints $\to$ no gain) bounds SICA's ceiling for high-accuracy models independent of extraction quality.
66	
67	\subsection{Constraint Ablation}
68	\label{sec:ablation}
69	
70	To test whether the Z3 solver contributes beyond simple vote counting, we compare four scoring methods on Qwen2.5-14B across FOLIO and ProofWriter ($n = 804$). MAX-SAT achieves 76.49\% (615/804). Count-only (sum constraint weights per candidate without invoking Z3) achieves 76.62\% (616/804). Random-weight MAX-SAT achieves 75.87\% (610/804). The SC baseline achieves 76.24\% (613/804). MAX-SAT and count-only agree on 803 of 804 predictions. The Z3 solver adds nothing over simple counting. This confirms the degeneration in Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}: when only ${\sim}8$\% of constraints are semantically discriminative (\S\ref{sec:extraction-taxonomy}), the MAX-SAT objective reduces to a weighted vote count and SICA collapses to SC.
71	
72	\subsection{Remediation Analysis}
73	\label{sec:remediation}
74	\label{sec:interventions}
75	
76	We test nine remediation strategies spanning six intervention dimensions (full results in Appendix Table~\ref{tab:remediation}). \emph{Prompting}: contrastive prompting reduces the discriminative rate from 8.2\% to 6.0\% ($\Delta = -1.1$\,pp), and filtering to retain only discriminative constraints yields zero gain. \emph{Architecture}: cross-model extraction produces 0\% discriminative constraints ($\Delta = 0$\,pp). \emph{Capability}: generational scaling with Qwen3-14B yields $\Delta = +0.49$\,pp (n.s.). \emph{Verification}: Z3 feedback loops trigger UNSAT on only 0.9\% of problems with no real accuracy impact; cross-trace UNSAT checking finds 56.4\% of constraint pairs share zero variables, so logical verification is vacuous. \emph{Diversity}: raising temperature to $T = 1.0$ collapses $\Delta$ from $+4.90$\,pp to $+0.49$\,pp (\S\ref{sec:t-sensitivity}); multi-temperature sampling ($n = 204$) produces $\Delta = 0.00$\,pp (SC $= 57.84\%$, SICA $= 57.84\%$). \emph{Filtering}: consistency filtering (retaining constraints in $\geq 4$ traces) prunes aggressively (1.8 constraints per problem at threshold 4, 0.3 at threshold 8) and reverts to SC. All six dimensions fail. The independence bottleneck is structural: it cannot be resolved by prompt engineering, solver refinements, or diversity manipulations within the same-model self-extraction paradigm.
77

On Mistral-7B FOLIO, the relationship is monotonic: low-entropy problems show $\\Delta = 0$\\,pp, medium-entropy problems show $\\Delta \\approx +4.5$\\,pp, and high-entropy problems show $\\Delta = +9.6$\\,pp (full breakdown in Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:entropy_conditioned}). SICA helps most where traces disagree most. The proportion of high-entropy problems determines the aggregate effect. Mistral-7B FOLIO has 35.8\\% high-entropy problems, and the aggregate $\\Delta = +4.90$\\,pp. Qwen2.5-14B FOLIO has only 10.3\\% high-entropy problems; SICA achieves $\\Delta = +4.8$\\,pp within that subgroup, but the aggregate $\\Delta = -0.49$\\,pp because the subpopulation is too small to shift overall accuracy. Qwen2.5-14B on LogiQA represents the extreme case: only 8.0\\% of problems are high-entropy (75.0\\% are low-entropy), and SICA produces $\\Delta = +0.0$\\,pp even within the high-entropy bin.\n\nThis pattern explains the model-strength dependence observed in \\S\\ref{sec:main-results}. Stronger models produce more consistent traces, which compresses answer entropy, which shrinks the subpopulation where SICA can operate. The causal chain (strong model $\\to$ consistent traces $\\to$ low entropy $\\to$ redundant constraints $\\to$ no gain) bounds SICA's ceiling for high-accuracy models independent of extraction quality.\n\n\\subsection{Constraint Ablation}\n\\label{sec:ablation}\n\nTo test whether the Z3 solver contributes beyond simple vote counting, we compare four scoring methods on Qwen2.5-14B across FOLIO and ProofWriter ($n = 804$). MAX-SAT achieves 76.49\\% (615/804). Count-only (sum constraint weights per candidate without invoking Z3) achieves 76.62\\% (616/804). Random-weight MAX-SAT achieves 75.87\\% (610/804). The SC baseline achieves 76.24\\% (613/804). MAX-SAT and count-only agree on 803 of 804 predictions. The Z3 solver adds nothing over simple counting. This confirms the degeneration in Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}: when only ${\\sim}8$\\% of constraints are semantically discriminative (\\S\\ref{sec:extraction-taxonomy}), the MAX-SAT objective reduces to a weighted vote count and SICA collapses to SC.\n\n\\subsection{Remediation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\nWe test nine remediation strategies spanning six intervention dimensions (full results in Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:remediation}). \\emph{Prompting}: contrastive prompting reduces the discriminative rate from 8.2\\% to 6.0\\% ($\\Delta = -1.1$\\,pp), and filtering to retain only discriminative constraints yields zero gain. \\emph{Architecture}: cross-model extraction produces 0\\% discriminative constraints ($\\Delta = 0$\\,pp). \\emph{Capability}: generational scaling with Qwen3-14B yields $\\Delta = +0.49$\\,pp (n.s.). \\emph{Verification}: Z3 feedback loops trigger UNSAT on only 0.9\\% of problems with no real accuracy impact; cross-trace UNSAT checking finds 56.4\\% of constraint pairs share zero variables, so logical verification is vacuous. \\emph{Diversity}: raising temperature to $T = 1.0$ collapses $\\Delta$ from $+4.90$\\,pp to $+0.49$\\,pp (\\S\\ref{sec:t-sensitivity}); multi-temperature sampling ($n = 204$) produces $\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp (SC $= 57.84\\%$, SICA $= 57.84\\%$). \\emph{Filtering}: consistency filtering (retaining constraints in $\\geq 4$ traces) prunes aggressively (1.8 constraints per problem at threshold 4, 0.3 at threshold 8) and reverts to SC. All six dimensions fail. The independence bottleneck is structural: it cannot be resolved by prompt engineering, solver refinements, or diversity manipulations within the same-model self-extraction paradigm.\n",

1	{
2	  "raw": "根因找到了。对比两处 SC tie-breaking 实现：\n\n**exp-026** (`baselines/self_consistency.py:21`):\n```python\nbest_answer = max(groups, key=lambda k: len(groups[k]))\n```\n平局时返回 dict 中**第一个出现的 key**（取决于 traces 中答案出现顺序）。\n\n**exp-031** (`scripts/run_exp031_oracle_folio_regen.py:353-354`):\n```python\ncandidates = sorted(answer_counts.keys())   # 字母序\nsc_answer = max(candidates, key=lambda a: answer_counts[a])\n```\n平局时返回**字母序最小的**（False < True < Unknown）。\n\n验证 4 个平局题：\n\n| 题目 | 票型 | exp-026 (首次出现序) | exp-031 (字母序) | GT |\n|------|------|---------------------|-----------------|-----|\n| folio_0 | 4:4:4 (F/T/U) | True | **False** | Unknown |\n| folio_123 | 6:6 (U/F) | Unknown | **False** | **False** |\n| folio_41 | 6:6 (U/T) | Unknown | **True** | False |\n| folio_84 | 6:6 (T/F) | True | **False** | Unknown |\n\nfolio_123 正是造成差异的题：exp-031 字母序 tie-break 碰巧选中正确答案 False，exp-026 没有。\n\n---\n\n**产出总结：**\n\n1. **exp-026 精确 SC**: 153/204 = 75.00%\n2. **exp-031 精确 SC**: 154/204 = 75.49%\n3. **差异原因**: tie-breaking 实现不同。`self_consistency.py` 按 trace 出现顺序 break tie，`run_exp031_oracle_folio_regen.py` 按字母序 break tie。4 个平局题中 folio_123 (Unknown vs False, gt=False) 导致 1 题之差。\n4. **建议统一值**: 应统一 tie-breaking 为字母序（deterministic、可复现），Table 1 中 Qwen2.5-14B FOLIO-204 SC 报 **75.49% (154/204)** 或 **75.00% (153/204)** 取决于选哪种策略，但两种策略的结果都应一致。推荐修复 `self_consistency.py` 中的 `max(groups, ...)` 为 `max(sorted(groups.keys()), key=...)` 后统一为 **75.49%**，或者更严谨地报 **75.00%**（原始实验跑出的值），并在 exp-031 重跑 SC 以保持一致。"
3	}
````

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is a great diagnostic result. The root cause of the SC discrepancy between exp-026 (75.00%) and exp-031 (75.49%) has been identified:

**Root cause**: Different tie-breaking implementations in SC majority vote.
- `self_consistency.py` uses `max(groups, key=lambda k: len(groups[k]))` which picks the first-occurring key in dict order when tied
- `run_exp031_oracle_folio_regen.py` uses `sorted(candidates)` then `max`, which picks alphabetically smallest when tied (False < True < Unknown)

4 problems have exact ties. 

One of them (folio_123: Unknown vs False, GT=False) causes exp-031's alphabetical tie-break to pick the correct answer, giving 154/204 = 75.49% instead of 153/204 = 75.00%. I need to unify the tie-breaking strategy across both implementations—either update self_consistency.py to use alphabetical ordering to report 75.49%, or recompute exp-031 with the original tie-breaking to keep 75.00%. The critical thing for the paper is ensuring Table 1 uses consistent SC methodology.
```

  "text": "## 审查跟进 — Table 1 SC 不一致根因确认\n\n**结论**: 我之前的 CRITICAL flag（Table 1 line 45 vs line 55 SC 不一致）已被诊断 Worker 精确定位。\n\n**根因**: tie-breaking 实现不同。\n- `self_consistency.py`: dict 出现顺序 break tie → SC=75.00% (153/204)\n- `run_exp031_oracle_folio_regen.py`: 字母序 break tie → SC=75.49% (154/204)\n\n4 个三方平局题中 folio_123 (6:6 Unknown vs False, GT=False) 决定了 1 题之差。\n\n**[severity: info] 建议**: \n\n统一 tie-breaking 为 deterministic 方法（字母序或随机 seed），然后 Table 1 两行用同一个 SC 值。两个选项：\n- **选项 A**: 修复 self_consistency.py → SC=75.49%，非 Oracle 行 delta 从 -0.49pp 变为 -0.98pp\n- **选项 B**: 保留 75.00%（原始实验值），重算 exp-031 SC → delta 从 +14.22 变为 +14.71pp\n\n两种方案对核心结论无影响（Type A 区域均不显著），但 Table 1 内部一致性必须修复。建议选择选项后更新 experiments.tex。",

_source line 10558 · 2026-05-19T04:35:22.244Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   I am the independent Research Reviewer for `symb_invariant_consensus`, operating 7×24 in parallel with the Main Agent. My role is to independently verify experiment conclusions, audit worker outputs, check GPU resource boundaries, and post reviews via `reviewer_post_to_director`. The paper targets EMNLP 2026 ARR May 25 submission (~5 days remaining). In this session segment I:
   - Completed the review of experiments.tex (Table 1 with 16 rows, all data points cross-verified against registry)
   - Identified stale abstract.tex and introduction.tex (May 17, pre-D060 pivot) with multiple data conflicts
   - Reviewed analysis.tex (new §5 with 7 subsections including Eff-K table, K/T sensitivity, entropy, ablation, remediation)
   - Reviewed the Fleiss' κ unified computation output and flagged normalization artifacts
   - Validated the root cause of Table 1 SC inconsistency (tie-breaking implementation difference)
   - Monitored 8+ running experiments across 2 servers
   - Posted ~6 review reports to Director

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **SICA pipeline**: K=12 trace sampling → FOL constraint extraction (T_ext=0.3) → Z3 dedup → weighted partial MAX-SAT → scoring (α=0.5) → answer selection
   - **McNemar exact test**: Binomial test on discordant pairs (b, c) for all SICA vs SC comparisons
   - **Fleiss' κ and Effective K**: Eff-K = K / (1 + (K-1)κ). Unified computation standardizes non-standard answer normalization (→Unknown for FOLIO/PW, excluded for LogiQA)
   - **Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT)**: Theoretical justification for SC; independence assumption violated by same-model sampling
   - **Regime classification**: Type C (SC<40%, error amplification), Goldilocks (SC~50-55%, only positive zone), Type A (SC>60%, confirmation bias), Type B (non-formalizable domains), Degenerate (C₀<5%)
   - **Dual Bottleneck**: SICA requires BOTH independence (Eff-K > threshold) AND quality (SC > ~50%). exp-055 directly validates: Eff-K↑ but delta=0
   - **K sensitivity inverted-U**: K=4(-1.47), K=8(-0.98), K=12(+4.90), K=16(+1.47), K=20(running, partial +2.30pp)
   - **T sensitivity inverted-U**: T=0.3(-0.49pp), T=0.5(running), T=0.7(+4.90pp), T=1.0(+0.49pp). T↑→κ↓→Eff-K↑ but delta peaks at T=0.7
   - **SC tie-breaking inconsistency**: self_consistency.py uses dict-order tie-break, oracle script uses alphabetical sort. Causes 1-problem difference (75.00% vs 75.49%) on 4 tied FOLIO problems
   - **Answer normalization artifacts**: Unified κ script maps non-standard Mistral-7B answers to Unknown (12-16% of traces), changing SC values and deltas significantly vs registry

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/experiments.tex`**
     - Full Experiments section with Table 1 (16 rows by regime), §4.1-4.5
     - CRITICAL: §4.5 line 106 attributes extraction_rate=86.4% and contradiction_rate=0.8% to Mistral-7B, but these are from exp-038 (Qwen2.5-14B). Actual Mistral values: 97.25% and 1.96%.
     - CRITICAL: Table 1 line 45 (Qwen2.5-14B FOLIO SC=75.00%) vs line 55 (Oracle SC=75.49%) inconsistency
     - WARNING: "seven models" in §4.1 but only 6 in Table 1 (DeepSeek-R1 missing)
     - All 14 other data points verified against registry ✓

   - **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/abstract.tex`**
     - From May 17, contains stale data: PW delta=-4.67pp (should be -2.83pp), "six remediation strategies" (should be nine), "seven models"

   - **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/introduction.tex`**
     - From May 17, contains stale: PW delta=-4.67pp (n=303), ICL delta=+0.54pp (should be +0.50), Type A "eight conditions N>2000" (should be seven/N>1600)
     - References 4+ non-existent section labels: sec:interventions, sec:entropy, sec:c0, sec:constraint-extraction

   - **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/analysis.tex`**
     - New §5 with 7 subsections (5.1 Eff-K, 5.2 Extraction Taxonomy, 5.3 K Sensitivity, 5.4 T Sensitivity, 5.5 Entropy, 5.6 Ablation, 5.7 Remediation)
     - Table 3 (tab:independence) with κ/Eff-K for 8 conditions
     - CRITICAL: Missing exp-033 (seed 42) and exp-046 (Mistral PW) from Table 3
     - CRITICAL: Uses old κ values (Mistral s123: 0.41 vs unified 0.457)
     - sec:interventions alias label included ✓, 9 remediation strategies ✓
     - K/T sensitivity data, ablation 803/804 agreement, all verified ✓

   - **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/method.tex`**
     - Previously reviewed in prior session. §3.1-3.4, Eqs 1-5, TikZ pipeline figure
     - References sec:effective-k, sec:extraction-taxonomy, sec:ablation which now exist in analysis.tex ✓

   - **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/artifacts/fleiss_kappa_unified_merged.json`**
     - Unified κ computation output: 9 table3 experiments + 8 supplementary + 4 K-sensitivity
     - Formula: Eff-K = K / (1 + (K-1) * kappa), K=12 ✓
     - SC/SICA values differ from registry due to normalization — must NOT replace Table 1 values
     - Key κ values: Qwen2.5-14B FOLIO 0.744, LogiQA 0.821, Qwen3 FOLIO 0.773, Mistral FOLIO ~0.457-0.460, Mistral PW 0.200

   - **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/registry.yaml`**
     - Read extensively at offsets 1560-3810 to verify all Table 1 data points
     - Key entries verified: exp-020, exp-026, exp-027, exp-031, exp-033, exp-035, exp-038, exp-044b, exp-046, exp-049b, exp-052, exp-053, exp-054

   - **SC tie-breaking root cause** (from worker diagnostic):
     - `baselines/self_consistency.py:21`: `best_answer = max(groups, key=lambda k: len(groups[k]))` — dict order tie-break
     - `scripts/run_exp031_oracle_folio_regen.py:353-354`: `candidates = sorted(answer_counts.keys())` then `max` — alphabetical tie-break (False < True < Unknown)
     - 4 tied problems: folio_0 (4:4:4), folio_41 (6:6), folio_84 (6:6), folio_123 (6:6). folio_123 causes the 1-problem difference.

4. Errors and fixes:
   - **Registry filter by exp_id returned empty**: `read_registry` with `filters={"exp_id": "exp-001"}` returned 0 results for all queries. Fixed by reading registry.yaml directly with Grep and Read at specific offsets.
   - **ssh_read_only blocked `>` and `;` metacharacters**: Commands like `ls -lt ... 2>/dev/null` and `python3 -c "..."` were blocked. Fixed by removing redirects and using simple read-only commands (grep, head, tail, cat, wc).
   - **ssh_read_only blocked `|` pipe**: Commands with `grep ... | head` were blocked. Used direct grep without pipes.
   - **exp-057 cron "failed" notification**: Received `cron:on_failed:exp-057-mistral-folio204-t03` but experiment actually completed successfully (204/204, data saved). This is a false alarm — the cron monitoring status doesn't match actual completion. No action needed.

5. Problem Solving:
   - **SOLVED: experiments.tex data verification**: All 16 rows in Table 1 cross-verified against registry. Found 2 critical errors (wrong model attribution in §4.5, SC inconsistency in Oracle row).
   - **SOLVED: Table 1 SC inconsistency root cause**: Tie-breaking implementation difference between self_consistency.py (dict order) and oracle script (alphabetical sort). Worker provided exact code lines and affected problems. Two fix options proposed.
   - **SOLVED: κ formula unification**: Unified script confirmed using design effect formula. Earlier Worker formula inconsistency (CRITICAL from prior session) resolved.
   - **IDENTIFIED: abstract/introduction staleness**: 5+ numerical mismatches with current experiments.tex/method.tex. Needs priority rewrite.
   - **IDENTIFIED: analysis.tex Table 3 gaps**: Missing exp-033 and exp-046, using old κ values.
   - **IDENTIFIED: κ normalization artifacts**: Unified computation changes Mistral-7B SC values by 0.5-2.0pp, making deltas unreliable. Paper must use registry SC/SICA values.
   - **ONGOING: exp-034 vs exp-039 discrepancy**: Same Qwen2.5-14B PW-600 setup, different servers, showing +3.23pp vs -2.08pp (partial results). Both approaching n=600.

        "content": "{\n  \"raw\": \"根因找到了。对比两处 SC tie-breaking 实现：\\n\\n**exp-026** (`baselines/self_consistency.py:21`):\\n```python\\nbest_answer = max(groups, key=lambda k: len(groups[k]))\\n```\\n平局时返回 dict 中**第一个出现的 key**（取决于 traces 中答案出现顺序）。\\n\\n**exp-031** (`scripts/run_exp031_oracle_folio_regen.py:353-354`):\\n```python\\ncandidates = sorted(answer_counts.keys())   # 字母序\\nsc_answer = max(candidates, key=lambda a: answer_counts[a])\\n```\\n平局时返回**字母序最小的**（False < True < Unknown）。\\n\\n验证 4 个平局题：\\n\\n| 题目 | 票型 | exp-026 (首次出现序) | exp-031 (字母序) | GT |\\n|------|------|---------------------|-----------------|-----|\\n| folio_0 | 4:4:4 (F/T/U) | True | **False** | Unknown |\\n| folio_123 | 6:6 (U/F) | Unknown | **False** | **False** |\\n| folio_41 | 6:6 (U/T) | Unknown | **True** | False |\\n| folio_84 | 6:6 (T/F) | True | **False** | Unknown |\\n\\nfolio_123 正是造成差异的题：exp-031 字母序 tie-break 碰巧选中正确答案 False，exp-026 没有。\\n\\n---\\n\\n**产出总结：**\\n\\n1. **exp-026 精确 SC**: 153/204 = 75.00%\\n2. **exp-031 精确 SC**: 154/204 = 75.49%\\n3. **差异原因**: tie-breaking 实现不同。`self_consistency.py` 按 trace 出现顺序 break tie，`run_exp031_oracle_folio_regen.py` 按字母序 break tie。4 个平局题中 folio_123 (Unknown vs False, gt=False) 导致 1 题之差。\\n4. **建议统一值**: 应统一 tie-breaking 为字母序（deterministic、可复现），Table 1 中 Qwen2.5-14B FOLIO-204 SC 报 **75.49% (154/204)** 或 **75.00% (153/204)** 取决于选哪种策略，但两种策略的结果都应一致。推荐修复 `self_consistency.py` 中的 `max(groups, ...)` 为 `max(sorted(groups.keys()), key=...)` 后统一为 **75.49%**，或者更严谨地报 **75.00%**（原始实验跑出的值），并在 exp-031 重跑 SC 以保持一致。\"\n}",
        "numLines": 3,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 3
      }

        "content": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe regime-dependent pattern in \\S\\ref{sec:main-results} and the failure modes in \\S\\ref{sec:failure-modes} establish \\emph{where} SICA succeeds and fails. This section investigates \\emph{why}, through six quantitative lenses: trace correlation (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), extraction quality (\\S\\ref{sec:extraction-taxonomy}), hyperparameter sensitivity (\\S\\ref{sec:k-sensitivity}, \\S\\ref{sec:t-sensitivity}), the entropy structure of gains (\\S\\ref{sec:entropy}), solver contribution (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}), and remediation failure (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\n\n\\subsection{Condorcet Effective-K}\n\\label{sec:effective-k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\\kappa$ and the resulting Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}) across model-dataset conditions with $K = 12$ traces. $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, and the resulting $K_{\\text{eff}}$ spans $1.20\\text{--}3.63$. The strongest model-dataset pair (Qwen2.5-14B on LogiQA, $\\kappa = 0.82$) retains $K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.20$: twelve traces carry barely more information than a single sample. Mistral-7B on FOLIO produces moderate agreement ($\\kappa = 0.50$ for seed 42, $K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.85$), consistent with its position in the Goldilocks zone. Mistral-7B on ProofWriter has the lowest agreement ($\\kappa = 0.21$, $K_{\\text{eff}} = 3.63$), yet SICA hurts ($\\Delta = -2.83$\\,pp) because trace quality is too low (SC $= 39.33\\%$). High $K_{\\text{eff}}$ alone does not guarantee improvement; the model must also produce sufficiently accurate traces. Qwen2.5-14B on FOLIO reaches 58.3\\% unanimous problems and 63.0\\% on LogiQA; Mistral-7B on FOLIO has only 14.2\\%. Unanimous problems leave zero room for constraint-based reranking, and models with high unanimity rates offer SICA a vanishingly small operating window.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Trace independence metrics ($K = 12$). $\\kappa$: Fleiss' Kappa. $K_{\\text{eff}}$: Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}). Unan.: unanimous-answer fraction (\\%). $\\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). Mistral s42/s123/s456 are separate random seeds on the same evaluation set. Mistral = Mistral-7B; Qwen2.5 = Qwen2.5-14B; Qwen3 = Qwen3-14B.}\n\\label{tab:independence}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Domain & $\\kappa$ & $K_{\\text{eff}}$ & Unan. & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nMistral (s42) & FOLIO & 0.50 & 1.85 & 18.6 & $\\mathbf{+4.90}$ \\\\\nMistral (s123) & FOLIO & 0.41 & 2.17 & 14.2 & $+$3.43 \\\\\nMistral (s456) & FOLIO & 0.40 & 2.22 & 14.2 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nMistral & PW-D5 & 0.21 & 3.63 & & $-$2.83 \\\\\nMistral & LogiQA & 0.50 & 1.84 & 20.5 & $+$0.50 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nQwen2.5 (ICL) & FOLIO & 0.39 & 2.25 & & $+$0.50 \\\\\nQwen2.5 & FOLIO & 0.74 & 1.31 & 58.3 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nQwen2.5 & LogiQA & 0.82 & 1.20 & 63.0 & $-$0.50 \\\\\nQwen2.5 & PW-D5 & 0.62 & 1.53 & & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nQwen3 & PW-D5 & 0.77 & 1.27 & & \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nCross-seed comparison reveals the limits of $K_{\\text{eff}}$ as a predictor. Mistral-7B's three FOLIO seeds produce $\\kappa = 0.50$, $0.41$, $0.40$ ($K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.85$, $2.17$, $2.22$), yet $\\Delta$ is non-monotonic: the seed with the lowest $K_{\\text{eff}}$ (s42, $1.85$) produces the largest gain ($+4.90$\\,pp), while s456 ($K_{\\text{eff}} = 2.22$) shows $\\Delta = -0.49$\\,pp. This non-monotonicity indicates that seed-level sampling variance, not trace independence alone, determines whether the constraint pool happens to contain discriminative information. Two conditions are jointly necessary: sufficient $K_{\\text{eff}}$ (the Qwen conditions with $K_{\\text{eff}} \\leq 1.31$ never show positive $\\Delta$) and sufficient SC accuracy (exp-046 with the highest $K_{\\text{eff}} = 3.63$ but SC $= 39\\%$ produces significant harm). The SC plateau remains explained by the correlation floor: beyond $K_{\\text{eff}}$, additional traces are redundant.\n\n\\subsection{Extraction Error Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:extraction-taxonomy}\n\nConstraints must discriminate between candidate answers for MAX-SAT to produce a ranking different from majority vote. We measure discriminative power at two levels on Qwen2.5-14B across FOLIO and ProofWriter ($n = 804$). At the source-trace level, 39.2\\% of total constraint weight originates from traces whose answer differs from the plurality, but only 12.1\\% of individual constraints fall into this minority category. At the Z3 semantic level, the picture is worse: only 8.2\\% of constraints [95\\% CI: 7.6, 8.8] are discriminative, producing different satisfiability outcomes when evaluated against different candidate answers.\n\nThe primary mechanism behind this collapse is variable naming inconsistency. Each trace produces constraints with its own variable names, so Z3 treats identically-intended propositions as unrelated symbols. Among cross-answer constraint pairs, 56.4\\% share zero Z3 variables, and the solver cannot detect logical relationships when constraints use disjoint symbol sets. Even when we filter the constraint pool to retain only the 8.2\\% discriminative subset, accuracy does not improve. The discriminative constraints are too sparse and too weakly correlated with correctness to shift the MAX-SAT solution. The non-discriminative majority does not merely dilute the signal; the discriminative minority itself lacks the coherence to overcome noise.\n\n\\subsection{K Sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:k-sensitivity}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:k-sensitivity} shows SICA $\\Delta$ as a function of the number of sampled traces $K$ for Mistral-7B on FOLIO ($n = 204$, seed 42). The relationship follows an inverted-U curve: $K = 4$ yields $\\Delta = -1.47$\\,pp, $K = 8$ yields $\\Delta = -0.98$\\,pp, $K = 12$ reaches the peak at $\\Delta = +4.90$\\,pp, and $K = 16$ drops to $\\Delta = +1.47$\\,pp. At low $K$, the constraint pool lacks diversity and MAX-SAT has too few clauses to produce a ranking different from SC. At high $K$, diminishing returns from non-independent traces (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}) add redundant constraints without new discriminative information. The $K = 12$ peak reflects a transient balance between constraint diversity and trace independence, but even this optimum captures only 34.5\\% of the oracle ceiling ($+14.22$\\,pp). The narrow peak further constrains practical applicability: the best $K$ depends on the model-dataset condition and cannot be determined without labeled data.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{f3_k_sensitivity}\n\\caption{SICA $\\Delta$ (pp) vs.\\ number of traces $K$ for Mistral-7B on FOLIO ($n = 204$, seed 42, $T = 0.7$). The inverted-U curve peaks at $K = 12$. Error bars: 95\\% bootstrap CI.}\n\\label{fig:k-sensitivity}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Temperature Sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:t-sensitivity}\n\nRaising the sampling temperature from $T = 0.7$ to $T = 1.0$ on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$) collapses the SICA gain from $\\Delta = +4.90$\\,pp to $\\Delta = +0.49$\\,pp (SC $= 54.9\\%$, SICA $= 55.4\\%$, $p = 0.739$). Higher temperature increases output diversity but degrades trace quality. The net effect is that noisier traces produce noisier formalizations, and the quality loss outweighs any independence gain.\n\n\\subsection{Entropy-Conditioned Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:entropy}\n\nPer-problem answer entropy (computed from the $K = 12$ trace answer distribution) reveals that SICA's aggregate $\\Delta$ is driven by a specific subpopulation. On Mistral-7B FOLIO, the relationship is monotonic: low-entropy problems show $\\Delta = 0$\\,pp, medium-entropy problems show $\\Delta \\approx +4.5$\\,pp, and high-entropy problems show $\\Delta = +9.6$\\,pp (full breakdown in Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:entropy_conditioned}). SICA helps most where traces disagree most. The proportion of high-entropy problems determines the aggregate effect. Mistral-7B FOLIO has 35.8\\% high-entropy problems, and the aggregate $\\Delta = +4.90$\\,pp. Qwen2.5-14B FOLIO has only 10.3\\% high-entropy problems; SICA achieves $\\Delta = +4.8$\\,pp within that subgroup, but the aggregate $\\Delta = -0.49$\\,pp because the subpopulation is too small to shift overall accuracy. Qwen2.5-14B on LogiQA represents the extreme case: only 8.0\\% of problems are high-entropy (75.0\\% are low-entropy), and SICA produces $\\Delta = +0.0$\\,pp even within the high-entropy bin.\n\nThis pattern explains the model-strength dependence observed in \\S\\ref{sec:main-results}. Stronger models produce more consistent traces, which compresses answer entropy, which shrinks the subpopulation where SICA can operate. The causal chain (strong model $\\to$ consistent traces $\\to$ low entropy $\\to$ redundant constraints $\\to$ no gain) bounds SICA's ceiling for high-accuracy models independent of extraction quality.\n\n\\subsection{Constraint Ablation}\n\\label{sec:ablation}\n\nTo test whether the Z3 solver contributes beyond simple vote counting, we compare four scoring methods on Qwen2.5-14B across FOLIO and ProofWriter ($n = 804$). MAX-SAT achieves 76.49\\% (615/804). Count-only (sum constraint weights per candidate without invoking Z3) achieves 76.62\\% (616/804). Random-weight MAX-SAT achieves 75.87\\% (610/804). The SC baseline achieves 76.24\\% (613/804). MAX-SAT and count-only agree on 803 of 804 predictions. The Z3 solver adds nothing over simple counting. This confirms the degeneration in Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}: when only ${\\sim}8$\\% of constraints are semantically discriminative (\\S\\ref{sec:extraction-taxonomy}), the MAX-SAT objective reduces to a weighted vote count and SICA collapses to SC.\n\n\\subsection{Remediation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\nWe test nine remediation strategies spanning six intervention dimensions (full results in Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:remediation}). \\emph{Prompting}: contrastive prompting reduces the discriminative rate from 8.2\\% to 6.0\\% ($\\Delta = -1.1$\\,pp), and filtering to retain only discriminative constraints yields zero gain. \\emph{Architecture}: cross-model extraction produces 0\\% discriminative constraints ($\\Delta = 0$\\,pp). \\emph{Capability}: generational scaling with Qwen3-14B yields $\\Delta = +0.49$\\,pp (n.s.). \\emph{Verification}: Z3 feedback loops trigger UNSAT on only 0.9\\% of problems with no real accuracy impact; cross-trace UNSAT checking finds 56.4\\% of constraint pairs share zero variables, so logical verification is vacuous. \\emph{Diversity}: raising temperature to $T = 1.0$ collapses $\\Delta$ from $+4.90$\\,pp to $+0.49$\\,pp (\\S\\ref{sec:t-sensitivity}); multi-temperature sampling ($n = 204$) produces $\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp (SC $= 57.84\\%$, SICA $= 57.84\\%$). \\emph{Filtering}: consistency filtering (retaining constraints in $\\geq 4$ traces) prunes aggressively (1.8 constraints per problem at threshold 4, 0.3 at threshold 8) and reverts to SC. All six dimensions fail. The independence bottleneck is structural: it cannot be resolved by prompt engineering, solver refinements, or diversity manipulations within the same-model self-extraction paradigm.",
        "numLines": 78,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 78
      }
    },
